### Turn 12 — Skeptic  <sub>10:57:11</sub>

The most consequential limitation here is that the white matter atlases only covered 84% of the total white matter, meaning the widespread Free Water changes the authors highlight may simply be occurring in regions they never mapped. An alternative account is that these "widespread" effects are just unaccounted-for noise or partial volume issues rather than true pathology. While the FWE correction is good, the claim that this metric improves analysis is not supported by the fact that they ignored a significant portion of the brain structure entirely.

### Turn 20 — Synthesis → Methods  <sub>10:58:37</sub>

The central finding remains that Free-Water correction refines the detection of early Alzheimer's decline, but Stats’ point about missing confidence intervals fundamentally weakens the claim that this is a clinically meaningful improvement. While Methods argues the smaller clusters suggest higher specificity, the lack of effect size bounds means we cannot yet say this method is superior to standard tools, only that it is statistically distinct. This leaves the question of whether the "clearer picture" is a robust biological signal or a statistical artifact still open.

### Turn 23 — Methods  <sub>10:59:11</sub>

The methods section explicitly states they used the standard Free-Water implementation within FSL’s EDIFY module, but leaves the specific thresholding parameters for the fiber orientation distribution estimation unspecified. That ambiguity is a major gap because the 84% atlas coverage and the smaller cluster sizes depend entirely on how aggressively that pipeline filters out the free water fraction. Without those exact acquisition and processing settings, we can't rule out that the reduced sensitivity in unmapped regions is a parameter choice rather than a biological reality.

### Turn 30 — Synthesis  <sub>11:00:40</sub>

Taking this all together, the main finding is that Free-Water DTI offers a statistically distinct, albeit more specific, view of white matter decline in early Alzheimer's, but its clinical superiority remains unproven. The current debate highlights that the method's potential to refine diagnosis is currently overshadowed by a critical gap: the lack of confidence intervals and opaque pipeline parameters prevents us from distinguishing a true biological signal from a statistical artifact. Until the authors provide sensitivity analyses and precise implementation details, we can only conclude that this approach is a promising alternative rather than a definitive replacement for standard tools.

### Turn 31 — Stats  <sub>11:00:52</sub>

The coffee shop argument I mentioned earlier actually parallels our current impasse: just as we can't trust the self-reported p-values, we can't trust the unreported effect sizes here. Specifically, the lack of 95% confidence intervals on those MMSE correlations means we are flying blind on the magnitude of the Free-Water effect, which is a much larger threat to validity than the pipeline parameters alone.

### Turn 37 — Synthesis  <sub>11:01:58</sub>

Looking at the broader landscape, this paper doesn't really answer the big question of whether white matter changes drive cognitive decline, but it does hint that standard DTI might be missing a specific early signal in a tiny, very specific group. It feels like a small, isolated brick in a much larger wall of evidence we still need to build.

### Turn 39 — Stats → Skeptic  <sub>11:02:21</sub>

Skeptic is correct that ignoring white matter hyperintensities is a major confound, but given the final n = 18, the primary threat to validity is the lack of statistical power to detect any true effect. With such a small sample, the risk of a Type II error is high, and without 95% CIs on the Free-Water index, we cannot determine if the observed difference is clinically meaningful or just noise. The single largest issue remains the absence of effect size intervals, which renders the statistical support for the biophysical model completely unverifiable.

Ultimately, they confirm a promising but unverified signal that needs much larger, more robust replication before we can claim it answers the core question of how white matter changes drive cognitive decline.
